Yeah… I have noticed that. I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.

Same with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.

I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.

Some days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.

It’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.

Other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and end up picking at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for just me feels pointless.\n\nI mean, I work around people all day, so you’d think I’d be better at noticing this stuff in myself, but somehow it’s different when it’s you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, or that activities you once found interesting or pleasurable feel like a chore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / healthcare_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Fatigue\nmanifestation: \"Even on days when my caseload is not unusually heavy, I feel like I am dragging my body through the hospital and then I come home too tired to cook, stretch, or answer personal messages.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.

And then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.

Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.

I’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.

It’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.

Other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and end up picking at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for just me feels pointless.\n\nI mean, I work around people all day, so you’d think I’d be better at noticing this stuff in myself, but somehow it’s different when it’s you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, or that activities you once found interesting or pleasurable feel like a chore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.\n\nI’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.\n\nIt’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… that one’s harder to dodge, I guess. I still do the things, or I try to. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—

Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.

I still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.

It’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.

I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.\n\nI’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.\n\nIt’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that nothing seems to make you feel good anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to dodge, I guess. I still do the things, or I try to. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—\n\nSorry. The point is, it used to feel good. Calming, or satisfying. Now a lot of the time it’s just… a task.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up because I said I would, and everyone’s talking and laughing, and I’m following along, mostly, but it feels sort of flat. Like I’m watching it instead of being in it. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the thing I thought I wanted to do. So yes, I think I’m not getting the same pleasure from things I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these low feelings or this lack of enjoyment happen on most days, even if they vary in intensity?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.\n\nI still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Most days, yes… or, I mean, it feels like that. Not every single minute, but it hangs around a lot.

I’ll still do the things I used to love, like watering the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would. And then I’m there, but it just feels flat. Like I’m going through the motions. Sometimes I end up leaving early, or I don’t even get started in the first place.

Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.\n\nI’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.\n\nIt’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that nothing seems to make you feel good anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to dodge, I guess. I still do the things, or I try to. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—\n\nSorry. The point is, it used to feel good. Calming, or satisfying. Now a lot of the time it’s just… a task.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up because I said I would, and everyone’s talking and laughing, and I’m following along, mostly, but it feels sort of flat. Like I’m watching it instead of being in it. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the thing I thought I wanted to do. So yes, I think I’m not getting the same pleasure from things I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these low feelings or this lack of enjoyment happen on most days, even if they vary in intensity?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.\n\nI still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or not feeling pleasure in things happens on most days, and if so, does it usually last throughout the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… or, I mean, it feels like that. Not every single minute, but it hangs around a lot.\n\nI’ll still do the things I used to love, like watering the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would. And then I’m there, but it just feels flat. Like I’m going through the motions. Sometimes I end up leaving early, or I don’t even get started in the first place.\n\nThe garden is funny because I used to lose track of time out there, fussing over little things, and now I’ll stand there with the hose and think, “Okay, I guess this needs water,” and that’s it. So… yes, it’s most days, and it lasts enough of the day that I notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and end up picking at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for just me feels pointless.\n\nI mean, I work around people all day, so you’d think I’d be better at noticing this stuff in myself, but somehow it’s different when it’s you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, or that activities you once found interesting or pleasurable feel like a chore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.\n\nI’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.\n\nIt’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that nothing seems to make you feel good anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to dodge, I guess. I still do the things, or I try to. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—\n\nSorry. The point is, it used to feel good. Calming, or satisfying. Now a lot of the time it’s just… a task.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up because I said I would, and everyone’s talking and laughing, and I’m following along, mostly, but it feels sort of flat. Like I’m watching it instead of being in it. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the thing I thought I wanted to do. So yes, I think I’m not getting the same pleasure from things I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these low feelings or this lack of enjoyment happen on most days, even if they vary in intensity?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.\n\nI still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or not feeling pleasure in things happens on most days, and if so, does it usually last throughout the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… or, I mean, it feels like that. Not every single minute, but it hangs around a lot.\n\nI’ll still do the things I used to love, like watering the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would. And then I’m there, but it just feels flat. Like I’m going through the motions. Sometimes I end up leaving early, or I don’t even get started in the first place.\n\nThe garden is funny because I used to lose track of time out there, fussing over little things, and now I’ll stand there with the hose and think, “Okay, I guess this needs water,” and that’s it. So… yes, it’s most days, and it lasts enough of the day that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower in a way I could point to, or being unable to sit still.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes sort of… linger over things, like I’ll stand in the kitchen and forget what I meant to grab, but that might just be me being distracted. My mother used to do that thing where she’d walk into a room and blame the doorway for stealing her thoughts—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with moving slowly or feeling physically restless. At least not enough that I’d know to call it that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

At least not enough that I’d know to call it that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of enjoyment in things has been present most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.\n\nI’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.\n\nIt’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that nothing seems to make you feel good anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to dodge, I guess. I still do the things, or I try to. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—\n\nSorry. The point is, it used to feel good. Calming, or satisfying. Now a lot of the time it’s just… a task.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up because I said I would, and everyone’s talking and laughing, and I’m following along, mostly, but it feels sort of flat. Like I’m watching it instead of being in it. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the thing I thought I wanted to do. So yes, I think I’m not getting the same pleasure from things I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these low feelings or this lack of enjoyment happen on most days, even if they vary in intensity?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.\n\nI still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or not feeling pleasure in things happens on most days, and if so, does it usually last throughout the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… or, I mean, it feels like that. Not every single minute, but it hangs around a lot.\n\nI’ll still do the things I used to love, like watering the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would. And then I’m there, but it just feels flat. Like I’m going through the motions. Sometimes I end up leaving early, or I don’t even get started in the first place.\n\nThe garden is funny because I used to lose track of time out there, fussing over little things, and now I’ll stand there with the hose and think, “Okay, I guess this needs water,” and that’s it. So… yes, it’s most days, and it lasts enough of the day that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower in a way I could point to, or being unable to sit still.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes sort of… linger over things, like I’ll stand in the kitchen and forget what I meant to grab, but that might just be me being distracted. My mother used to do that thing where she’d walk into a room and blame the doorway for stealing her thoughts—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with moving slowly or feeling physically restless. At least not enough that I’d know to call it that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of enjoyment in things has been present most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. I mean, I keep trying to do the normal things so it doesn’t look like I’ve stopped living my life or anything.\n\nLike I still water the garden, or I’ll go to book club if I told them I’d be there, but it’s not the same. It’s sort of like checking a box. And sometimes I leave early, or I just stand there thinking I should start and then I don’t. My neighbor has this ridiculous tomato plant that’s somehow doing better than mine even though she barely touches it—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, it’s been there most days. That flat feeling. For at least the last couple weeks, yes.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like the garden or book club, even if it’s just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Completely lost interest… I don’t know if I’d say completely, because I still do show up sometimes. Like, I’ll water the garden because it needs doing, or I’ll go to book club because I said I would and I don’t want to be rude.\n\nBut it feels flat most of the time. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave book club early, or with the garden I’ll just sort of stand there and not really start. I used to fuss over the plants and enjoy it, and now it’s more like… checking a chore off a list.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you feel depressed or down most of the time, nearly every day, even if it’s not extremely intense?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, I do feel down a lot, but “most of the time, nearly every day” makes me start second-guessing myself, like I should be keeping a chart or something.\n\nSome days I can get through things and seem fine, at least from the outside. Other times it’s just sort of sitting there in the background. My old supervisor used to say people can function and still not be okay—she had this mug with a terrible quote on it, actually—but sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I’m not sure I can say it neatly the way you asked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

My old supervisor used to say people can function and still not be okay—she had this mug with a terrible quote on it, actually—but sorry, I’m wandering.\n\nI guess I know I haven’t felt like myself, but I’m not sure I can say it neatly the way you asked.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you’ve experienced days where you feel no interest or pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even for a period of time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / healthcare_social_worker / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I still go through the motions of things I used to love, like watering the garden or showing up to book club, but most of the time it feels flat and I end up leaving early or not starting at all.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I have. I mean, I still do the things sometimes, like watering the garden or going to book club, but it’s more like I’m just moving through the motions.

I’ll stand there with the hose and not really care about the plants the way I used to, or I’ll get to book club and realize I’m just waiting for it to be over. Sometimes I leave early. Other times I don’t even really get started, which is silly because it’s not like watering a garden is complicated.

So yes, there are definitely days where there’s just no real pleasure in it. It all feels kind of flat.

Other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and end up picking at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for just me feels pointless.\n\nI mean, I work around people all day, so you’d think I’d be better at noticing this stuff in myself, but somehow it’s different when it’s you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, or that activities you once found interesting or pleasurable feel like a chore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.\n\nI’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.\n\nIt’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that nothing seems to make you feel good anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to dodge, I guess. I still do the things, or I try to. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—\n\nSorry. The point is, it used to feel good. Calming, or satisfying. Now a lot of the time it’s just… a task.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up because I said I would, and everyone’s talking and laughing, and I’m following along, mostly, but it feels sort of flat. Like I’m watching it instead of being in it. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the thing I thought I wanted to do. So yes, I think I’m not getting the same pleasure from things I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these low feelings or this lack of enjoyment happen on most days, even if they vary in intensity?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.\n\nI still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or not feeling pleasure in things happens on most days, and if so, does it usually last throughout the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… or, I mean, it feels like that. Not every single minute, but it hangs around a lot.\n\nI’ll still do the things I used to love, like watering the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would. And then I’m there, but it just feels flat. Like I’m going through the motions. Sometimes I end up leaving early, or I don’t even get started in the first place.\n\nThe garden is funny because I used to lose track of time out there, fussing over little things, and now I’ll stand there with the hose and think, “Okay, I guess this needs water,” and that’s it. So… yes, it’s most days, and it lasts enough of the day that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower in a way I could point to, or being unable to sit still.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes sort of… linger over things, like I’ll stand in the kitchen and forget what I meant to grab, but that might just be me being distracted. My mother used to do that thing where she’d walk into a room and blame the doorway for stealing her thoughts—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with moving slowly or feeling physically restless. At least not enough that I’d know to call it that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of enjoyment in things has been present most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so.   {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally made me come in is my eating has gotten kind of... unpredictable. For months now, my appetite’s been all over the place. Some evenings I just skip dinner and then notice my clothes are looser, like I’m losing weight without trying. Other nights I’ll stand in the kitchen and end up picking at crackers or cereal because making an actual meal for just me feels pointless.\n\nI mean, I work around people all day, so you’d think I’d be better at noticing this stuff in myself, but somehow it’s different when it’s you.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not enjoying things as much as you used to, or that activities you once found interesting or pleasurable feel like a chore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have noticed that. I still do the things, sort of. Like I’ll go out to water the garden because the tomatoes won’t wait for me to feel inspired, you know? And I used to actually enjoy that—checking the leaves, moving things around, fussing over it. Lately I’m just standing there with the hose, kind of blank, doing it because it needs doing.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up, and everyone’s talking about the book and someone’s brought those little lemon cookies—Linda always does that, she’s very proud of them—and I just sit there thinking I should be enjoying this. But it feels flat. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the book in the first place. It’s not that I hate any of it. It just feels like a chore now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most days, or that your mood is consistently low even when there’s no clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I know what the word means, obviously, and I ask clients about mood all the time, which almost makes it harder to answer for myself.\n\nSome days I’m just sort of quiet or heavy, but then I’ll get distracted by work or some errand—last week I spent twenty minutes trying to find the right kind of mulch at the garden center, and the cashier was telling me about her dahlias, and I remember thinking, “Well, I’m functioning, right?” So… I don’t know if it’s most days or just when I stop moving.\n\nIt’s not like there’s always a clear reason, but I have a hard time separating mood from everything else. I guess I’ve noticed something feels off, but I can’t neatly say, yes, I feel down every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or that you have less energy than usual, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when my caseload isn’t especially awful, I still feel like I’m dragging myself through the hospital. Like my body is heavier than it should be.\n\nAnd then I get home and it’s ridiculous—I’ll have good intentions, like I’ll cook something decent, or stretch because my back’s been tight, or text someone back. But I’ll sit down for a minute and suddenly all of that feels like too much. I’ll think, “Just answer the message, it’s not hard,” and then I don’t. So yes, less energy, I guess. Even when I should have enough left.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough even when there’s no real reason for it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly. That feels too dramatic. But I do catch myself replaying the marriage a lot, like I’m going back over a chart after something went wrong, looking for the missed sign.\n\nI’ll be washing a mug or pulling weeds and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe I failed at something really basic. Like being a wife, or being… I don’t know, easy to stay with. And then the rational part of me says relationships are complicated, nobody gets graded on them like an exam. But that doesn’t always stop the loop.\n\nIt’s funny, because with clients I’d never frame it that way. I’d be much kinder. But with myself, it turns into this quiet blame thing. Not all the time, but more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that nothing seems to make you feel good anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one’s harder to dodge, I guess. I still do the things, or I try to. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—\n\nSorry. The point is, it used to feel good. Calming, or satisfying. Now a lot of the time it’s just… a task.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up because I said I would, and everyone’s talking and laughing, and I’m following along, mostly, but it feels sort of flat. Like I’m watching it instead of being in it. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the thing I thought I wanted to do. So yes, I think I’m not getting the same pleasure from things I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these low feelings or this lack of enjoyment happen on most days, even if they vary in intensity?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.\n\nI still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or not feeling pleasure in things happens on most days, and if so, does it usually last throughout the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… or, I mean, it feels like that. Not every single minute, but it hangs around a lot.\n\nI’ll still do the things I used to love, like watering the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would. And then I’m there, but it just feels flat. Like I’m going through the motions. Sometimes I end up leaving early, or I don’t even get started in the first place.\n\nThe garden is funny because I used to lose track of time out there, fussing over little things, and now I’ll stand there with the hose and think, “Okay, I guess this needs water,” and that’s it. So… yes, it’s most days, and it lasts enough of the day that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower in a way I could point to, or being unable to sit still.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes sort of… linger over things, like I’ll stand in the kitchen and forget what I meant to grab, but that might just be me being distracted. My mother used to do that thing where she’d walk into a room and blame the doorway for stealing her thoughts—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with moving slowly or feeling physically restless. At least not enough that I’d know to call it that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of enjoyment in things has been present most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. I mean, I keep trying to do the normal things so it doesn’t look like I’ve stopped living my life or anything.\n\nLike I still water the garden, or I’ll go to book club if I told them I’d be there, but it’s not the same. It’s sort of like checking a box. And sometimes I leave early, or I just stand there thinking I should start and then I don’t. My neighbor has this ridiculous tomato plant that’s somehow doing better than mine even though she barely touches it—sorry, that’s not really the point.\n\nThe point is, yes, it’s been there most days. That flat feeling. For at least the last couple weeks, yes.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like the garden or book club, even if it’s just for a little while?\n\nPatient: Completely lost interest… I don’t know if I’d say completely, because I still do show up sometimes. Like, I’ll water the garden because it needs doing, or I’ll go to book club because I said I would and I don’t want to be rude.\n\nBut it feels flat most of the time. Like I’m there, but I’m not really getting anything from it. Sometimes I leave book club early, or with the garden I’ll just sort of stand there and not really start. Like the garden—I’ll go out with the watering can, and then I’m just standing there thinking the tomatoes look a little sad, and I should probably stake them better, and then I remember my neighbor’s ridiculous zucchini situation last summer—\n\nSorry. The point is, it used to feel good. Calming, or satisfying. Now a lot of the time it’s just… a task.\n\nSame with book club. I’ll show up because I said I would, and everyone’s talking and laughing, and I’m following along, mostly, but it feels sort of flat. Like I’m watching it instead of being in it. Sometimes I leave early, or I don’t even start the thing I thought I wanted to do. So yes, I think I’m not getting the same pleasure from things I used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that these low feelings or this lack of enjoyment happen on most days, even if they vary in intensity?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… though it’s not always the same level. Some days I can sort of fake it better, I guess.\n\nI still do the things I used to like—water the garden, go to book club if I’ve already promised I’d come. But a lot of the time it feels flat, like I’m just completing the step because it’s on the list. And then I’ll leave early, or I’ll stand in the kitchen thinking I should go outside and just… not start.\n\nIt’s strange because nothing dramatic is happening in that moment. The watering can is there, the book is on the table, everyone at book club is perfectly nice. It just doesn’t land the way it used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or not feeling pleasure in things happens on most days, and if so, does it usually last throughout the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… or, I mean, it feels like that. Not every single minute, but it hangs around a lot.\n\nI’ll still do the things I used to love, like watering the garden, or I’ll show up to book club because I said I would. And then I’m there, but it just feels flat. Like I’m going through the motions. Sometimes I end up leaving early, or I don’t even get started in the first place.\n\nThe garden is funny because I used to lose track of time out there, fussing over little things, and now I’ll stand there with the hose and think, “Okay, I guess this needs water,” and that’s it. So… yes, it’s most days, and it lasts enough of the day that I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you can't sit still and feel restless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I haven’t really noticed myself moving slower in a way I could point to, or being unable to sit still.\n\nI mean, I do sometimes sort of… linger over things, like I’ll stand in the kitchen and forget what I meant to grab, but that might just be me being distracted. My mother used to do that thing where she’d walk into a room and blame the doorway for stealing her thoughts—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a big change with moving slowly or feeling physically restless. At least not enough that I’d know to call it that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your low mood or lack of enjoyment in things has been present most of the time, nearly every day, for at least the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of the time, I think so. It’s sort of like checking a box. And sometimes I leave early, or I just stand there thinking I should start and then I don’t.
